    "newString": " and showed that MFRM separates genuine ability from systematic judge effects in multi-judge panels.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Confounding in LLM Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:confounding}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_ovb_bias}\n\\caption{Sequential estimation bias ($\\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{seq}} - \\theta_{\\mathrm{true}}$) versus true contamination susceptibility $c_n$. Pearson $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$) confirms systematic overestimation of contaminated models. Synthetic data: $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$.}\n\\label{fig:ovb_bias}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) extends classical Rasch measurement to settings with multiple rater facets \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm}. Originating in educational measurement, the model treats rater variation as a nuisance facet to be estimated and removed rather than a source of noise to be averaged over. In the LLM evaluation context, where $J$ judges score $N$ models across $I$ items, the MFRM specifies\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mfrm}\n\\log \\frac{P(Y_{nij} \\geq k \\mid \\theta_n, b_i, d_j, \\gamma_k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k \\mid \\theta_n, b_i, d_j, \\gamma_k)} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\gamma_k,\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $Y_{nij}$ is the ordinal rating by judge $j$ for model $n$ on item $i$, $\\theta_n$ is the ability of model $n$ ($n = 1, \\ldots, N$), $b_i$ is the difficulty of item $i$ ($i = 1, \\ldots, I$), $d_j$ is the severity of judge $j$ ($j = 1, \\ldots, J$), and $\\gamma_k$ is the threshold for category $k$. All facets reside on a common logit scale, so model ability is directly comparable after adjustment for item difficulty and judge severity. \\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} applied this framework to compare ten LLM raters with human experts, showing that MFRM separates genuine ability from systematic judge effects in multi-judge panels.\n\nHowever, standard MFRM assumes that all systematic sources of score variation are captured by the modeled facets. This assumption breaks down when some models have been exposed to benchmark items during training \\citep{dcr2025}. Such contamination inflates scores on exposed items through memorization rather than genuine ability. We represent this effect as an interaction $c_n \\cdot e_i$, where $c_n \\geq 0$ is the contamination susceptibility of model $n$ and $e_i \\in [0, 1]$ is the exposure level of item $i$. The complete data-generating process becomes\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:full}\n\\eta_{nijk} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\gamma_k + c_n \\cdot e_i.\n\\end{equation}\n\n\nWhen a practitioner fits the standard MFRM (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}) to data generated by Eq.~\\ref{eq:full}, the contamination term is absent from the estimated model. Its influence does not vanish but is absorbed into the estimated parameters $\\tilde{\\theta}_n$, $\\tilde{b}_i$, and $\\tilde{d}_j$, which distorts all downstream inferences.\n\nThe consequence of this omission can be stated precisely.\n\n\\begin{observation}[Omitted Variable Bias in MFRM]\n\\label{obs:ovb}\nLet the full model be $\\eta_{nijk} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\gamma_k + c_n \\cdot e_i$ and the short model omit the contamination term: $\\tilde{\\eta}_{nijk} = \\tilde{\\theta}_n - \\tilde{b}_i - \\tilde{d}_j - \\gamma_k$. Under a linear approximation to the ordinal logit link \\citep{wooldridge2010, angrist2009mostly},\\footnote{The OVB formula is exact for linear models. We apply it to the MFRM linear predictor $\\eta$, which is an approximation. Synthetic validation confirms the approximation quality: the Pearson correlation between the predicted bias direction and empirical bias reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), indicating that the linear result accurately characterizes bias direction and relative magnitude.}\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:ovb}\n\\mathrm{plim}(\\tilde{\\theta}_n - \\theta_n) = \\delta_n \\cdot c_n,\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\delta_n$ depends on the projection of the exposure vector $(e_i)$ onto the design matrix of the short model (explicit form in Appendix~\\ref{app:gamma}). The bias is non-zero whenever $\\mathrm{Cov}(d_j,\\, c_n \\cdot e_i) \\neq 0$. This captures the confounding component; in the nonlinear MFRM, a specification bias from omitting $c_n \\cdot e_i$ persists even at zero covariance (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{observation}\n\nWhile the OVB framework provides the analytical lens for understanding \\emph{why} omitting contamination biases ability estimates, our empirical results (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}) reveal that the bias magnitude is driven primarily by the specification error itself rather than the exposure-severity covariance term---a finding that strengthens the case for joint modeling regardless of the covariance structure. G-MFRM uses the full nonlinear likelihood; the linear approximation serves only as directional motivation. Monte Carlo simulation confirms that bias magnitude is approximately constant across confounding levels $\\rho$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_vs_rho}), indicating that omission of $c_n \\cdot e_i$, not its correlation with $d_j$, is the primary driver. Joint estimation reduces the bias slope by ${\\sim}$69\\% at all $\\rho$ levels. Neither sequential ordering resolves this: bias-first approaches \\citep{cyclicjudge2026} absorb contamination into $\\hat{\\theta}$, while contamination-first approaches \\citep{dcr2025} misattribute judge severity as contamination ($r = 0.86$, $p < 0.001$; Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_bias_vs_rho}\n\\caption{Sequential estimation bias is approximately constant across confounding levels $\\rho$, confirming that omission of the contamination term, not its correlation with judge severity, drives the bias. Joint G-MFRM reduces the bias slope by ${\\sim}$69\\% at all $\\rho$ levels. Synthetic data: $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$.}\n\\label{fig:bias_vs_rho}\n\\end{figure}",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_contamination_bars}\n\\caption{Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ (posterior mean $\\pm$ 95\\% CI), ordered by magnitude ($N = 8$ pilot, 9 judges). Older models (GPT-3.5-Turbo, GPT-4-0613) show 4--6$\\times$ higher susceptibility than recent models (GPT-4o).}\n\\label{fig:contamination_bars}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nOur main experiment uses $N = 17$ target models evaluated by 6 LLM judges; a smaller $N = 8$ pilot with 9 judges validates sample-size effects. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} reports both configurations as part of the coverage analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \\citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 500 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. The evaluation uses all 500 items in a connected incomplete design (${\\sim}$58\\% fill rate of the $N \\times I \\times J$ tensor). Seventeen target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated: GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini, Qwen2.5-72B, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen3-8B, Gemma-2-9B, GPT-4-0613, GPT-3.5-Turbo, Claude-3.5-Sonnet, GPT-4-Turbo, LLaMA-3.1-70B, Gemma-2-27B, Claude-3-Opus, LLaMA-3-70B, Mixtral-8x22B, Claude-3-Haiku, and LLaMA-3-8B. Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}.\n\n\\paragraph{Judges.} Six LLM judges score responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Four provide complete item coverage; two (Gemini, Sonnet) cover ${\\sim}$63--79\\% of items, yielding an incomplete connected design. Two models serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs self-evaluation bias ($\\tau$ unchanged when excluding self-judging; Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item $e_i$ follows the two-source pipeline in \\S\\ref{sec:staged} (75\\% reference anchoring, 25\\% cross-model agreement; robust to weight configuration, $\\rho \\geq 0.998$; Appendix~\\ref{app:weight_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{MCMC.} NumPyro NUTS, 6{,}000 warmup $+$ 4{,}000 samples $\\times$ 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). Convergence: $\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$ \\citep{vehtari2021rhat, gelman2013bda}.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Item Set} & \\textbf{Items} & \\textbf{Cov.} & $\\tau_{\\mathrm{raw}}$ & $\\tau_{\\mathrm{G\\text{-}MFRM}}$ & $\\Delta\\tau$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{$N = 17$, 6 judges (main experiment)}} \\\\\nFull set & 500 & 46\\% & 0.838 & 0.794 & $-$0.044 \\\\\nFiltered & 317 & 73\\% & 0.824 & 0.809 & $-$0.015 \\\\\nExposed-only & 231 & 100\\% & 0.779 & 0.779 & \\phantom{$-$}0.000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{$N = 8$, 9 judges (pilot)}} \\\\\nFull set & 176 & -- & 0.786 & 0.857$^{\\dagger}$ & $+$0.071 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Ranking accuracy (Kendall $\\tau$ against Chatbot Arena) as a function of exposure coverage. G-MFRM over-correction decreases across all three coverage levels ($\\Delta\\tau$: $-0.044 \\to -0.015 \\to 0.000$). At full coverage, G-MFRM matches the raw average exactly; the pilot ($N = 8$) suggests positive $\\Delta\\tau$ when coverage is adequate. All seven baselines (MFRM-only, Sequential, CyclicJudge, DCR Factor, Pref.\\ Leakage) produce $\\tau$ within 0.014 of the raw average at both panel sizes. $^{\\dagger}$Bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nWe compare G-MFRM against seven baselines that address judge bias or contamination separately (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}): Raw Average, MFRM-only (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}), Sequential (MFRM then contamination adjustment), CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, CyclicJudge+discard, DCR Factor \\citep{dcr2025}, and Preference Leakage calibration \\citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\\tau$ and Spearman $\\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.\n\n\\paragraph{Synthetic validation.} On synthetic data ($N = 50$, 20 seeds), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 10^{-6}$) and improves ranking accuracy ($\\tau$: $0.922$ vs.\\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). Contamination susceptibility is recovered with Pearson $r = 0.81$.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictive fit.} In the $N = 8$ pilot, LOO cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM over MFRM-only (Expected Log Pointwise Predictive Density difference $\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, SE $= 9.4$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 and all Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:loo_comparison}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reconciling predictive fit and ranking accuracy.} The LOO improvement and the ranking degradation are not contradictory: they measure different quantities. LOO-ELPD evaluates observation-level predictive accuracy across all $(n, i, j)$ triples, rewarding the model for capturing contamination structure in individual responses. Kendall $\\tau$ evaluates aggregate system-level rankings, which are sensitive to the accuracy of per-item exposure estimates $e_i$. The separation indicates that the contamination signal is real (the model fits better), but incomplete exposure coverage causes the correction to over-correct when aggregated to rankings. The coverage ablation below confirms this interpretation.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure coverage and ranking accuracy.} G-MFRM's ranking accuracy on real data depends on exposure estimation quality. At $N = 17$, the full item set (500 items) has only 46\\% of items with non-zero exposure estimates; G-MFRM over-corrects on the remaining items, yielding $\\Delta\\tau = -0.044$ relative to the raw average ($\\tau = 0.794$ vs.\\ $0.838$). We trace this degradation through three item subsets with increasing exposure coverage:\n%\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Full set (500 items, 46\\% $e_i > 0$): $\\Delta\\tau = -0.044$\n    \\item Filtered set (317 items, 73\\% $e_i > 0$): $\\Delta\\tau = -0.015$\n    \\item Exposed-only (231 items, 100\\% $e_i > 0$): $\\Delta\\tau = 0.000$\n\\end{itemize}\n%\nThis controlled ablation ($-0.044 \\to -0.015 \\to 0.000$) demonstrates that over-correction is attributable to incomplete exposure coverage. Two additional variants confirm the mechanism: MFRM-only on the filtered set yields $\\Delta\\tau = 0.000$, isolating the degradation to the contamination gating term; a contamination-only variant without severity modeling yields $\\Delta\\tau = -0.118$, demonstrating that naive linear contamination deduction without joint calibration is catastrophically harmful.\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination diagnostics.} G-MFRM's primary real-data contribution is interpretable per-model contamination estimation. In the $N = 8$ pilot, the contamination susceptibility profile (Figure~\\ref{fig:contamination_bars}) reveals a clear gradient by model age: older models show $\\hat{c}_n > 4$ (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $5.60$; GPT-4-0613: $4.20$), while recent models show near-zero susceptibility (GPT-4o: $0.88$; GPT-4o-mini: $0.93$). MFRM-only estimation absorbs this contamination signal into ability estimates, inflating $\\hat{\\theta}$ for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_absorption}; Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$ between $\\hat{c}_n$ and $\\Delta\\theta$, though with only $N = 8$ data points, perfect rank correlation is not statistically remarkable and should not be interpreted as a robust finding). The $N = 17$ analysis preserves the same age gradient at lower absolute scale (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $2.57$; GPT-4-0613: $3.78$), consistent with shrinkage from the larger model population. All parameters achieve $\\hat{R} \\leq 1.003$ with ESS $\\geq 1{,}240$ in the $N = 8$ pilot (Appendices~\\ref{app:item_diagnostics} and~\\ref{app:ppc}).\n\n\\paragraph{Judge severity and panel design.} Judge severity profiles vary across the panel (Figure~\\ref{fig:judge_severity}), and contamination--ability separation improves with severity diversity: panels with $k \\geq 4$ judges covering the severity extremes provide the most reliable diagnostic output (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig6_bias_absorption}\n\\caption{Ability bias absorbed by MFRM-only estimation ($N = 8$ pilot): $\\Delta\\theta = \\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{MFRM}} - \\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{G\\text{-}MFRM}}$. The strong correlation with $\\hat{c}_n$ (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$; $N = 8$, interpret with caution) confirms that contamination inflates MFRM ability estimates for high-$c_n$ models, as predicted by the OVB analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).}\n\\label{fig:bias_absorption}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation and Ablation}\n\\label{sec:error_prop_results}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ablation_rmse}\n\\caption{Ablation on synthetic data ($N = 50$, 20 seeds). Joint estimation drives the improvement: staged estimation doubles $\\theta$-RMSE ($p < 0.001$). Continuous gating provides a moderate gain over binary ($p < 0.001$); removing judge severity $d_j$ has negligible effect on ability recovery (n.s.).}\n\\label{fig:ablation}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} validates the theoretical prediction (\\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}): below $\\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\\%; beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial. Ablation (Figure~\\ref{fig:ablation}; full results in Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation}) confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor ($2.0\\times$ $\\theta$-RMSE increase under staged estimation, $p < 0.001$) and continuous gating over binary ($c_n$-RMSE $-$40\\%, $p < 0.001$).",

All seven baselines (MFRM-only, Sequential, CyclicJudge, DCR Factor, Pref.\\ Leakage) produce $\\tau$ within 0.014 of the raw average at both panel sizes. $^{\\dagger}$Bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nWe compare G-MFRM against seven baselines that address judge bias or contamination separately (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}): Raw Average, MFRM-only (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}), Sequential (MFRM then contamination adjustment), CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, CyclicJudge+discard, DCR Factor \\citep{dcr2025}, and Preference Leakage calibration \\citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\\tau$ and Spearman $\\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.\n\n\\paragraph{Synthetic validation.} On synthetic data ($N = 50$, 20 seeds), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 10^{-6}$) and improves ranking accuracy ($\\tau$: $0.922$ vs.\\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). Contamination susceptibility is recovered with Pearson $r = 0.81$.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictive fit.} In the $N = 8$ pilot, LOO cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM over MFRM-only (Expected Log Pointwise Predictive Density difference $\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, SE $= 9.4$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 and all Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:loo_comparison}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reconciling predictive fit and ranking accuracy.} The LOO improvement and the ranking degradation are not contradictory: they measure different quantities. LOO-ELPD evaluates observation-level predictive accuracy across all $(n, i, j)$ triples: it rewards the model for capturing contamination structure in individual responses. Kendall $\\tau$ evaluates aggregate system-level rankings, which are sensitive to the accuracy of per-item exposure estimates $e_i$. The separation indicates that the contamination signal is real (the model fits better), but incomplete exposure coverage causes the correction to over-correct when aggregated to rankings. The coverage ablation below confirms this interpretation.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure coverage and ranking accuracy.} G-MFRM's ranking accuracy on real data depends on exposure estimation quality. At $N = 17$, the full item set (500 items) has only 46\\% of items with non-zero exposure estimates; G-MFRM over-corrects on the remaining items, and the result is $\\Delta\\tau = -0.044$ relative to the raw average ($\\tau = 0.794$ vs.\\ $0.838$). We trace this degradation through three item subsets with increasing exposure coverage:\n%\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Full set (500 items, 46\\% $e_i > 0$): $\\Delta\\tau = -0.044$\n    \\item Filtered set (317 items, 73\\% $e_i > 0$): $\\Delta\\tau = -0.015$\n    \\item Exposed-only (231 items, 100\\% $e_i > 0$): $\\Delta\\tau = 0.000$\n\\end{itemize}\n%\nThis controlled ablation ($-0.044 \\to -0.015 \\to 0.000$) demonstrates that over-correction is attributable to incomplete exposure coverage. Two additional variants confirm the mechanism: MFRM-only on the filtered set yields $\\Delta\\tau = 0.000$, isolating the degradation to the contamination gating term; a contamination-only variant without severity modeling yields $\\Delta\\tau = -0.118$, demonstrating that naive linear contamination deduction without joint calibration is catastrophically harmful.\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination diagnostics.} G-MFRM's primary real-data contribution is interpretable per-model contamination estimation. In the $N = 8$ pilot, the contamination susceptibility profile (Figure~\\ref{fig:contamination_bars}) reveals a clear gradient by model age: older models show $\\hat{c}_n > 4$ (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $5.60$; GPT-4-0613: $4.20$), while recent models show near-zero susceptibility (GPT-4o: $0.88$; GPT-4o-mini: $0.93$). MFRM-only estimation absorbs this contamination signal into ability estimates, inflating $\\hat{\\theta}$ for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_absorption}; Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$ between $\\hat{c}_n$ and $\\Delta\\theta$, though with only $N = 8$ data points, perfect rank correlation is not statistically remarkable and should not be interpreted as a robust finding). The $N = 17$ analysis preserves the same age gradient at lower absolute scale (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $2.57$; GPT-4-0613: $3.78$), consistent with shrinkage from the larger model population. All parameters achieve $\\hat{R} \\leq 1.003$ with ESS $\\geq 1{,}240$ in the $N = 8$ pilot (Appendices~\\ref{app:item_diagnostics} and~\\ref{app:ppc}).\n\n\\paragraph{Judge severity and panel design.} Judge severity profiles vary across the panel (Figure~\\ref{fig:judge_severity}), and contamination--ability separation improves with severity diversity: panels with $k \\geq 4$ judges covering the severity extremes provide the most reliable diagnostic output (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig6_bias_absorption}\n\\caption{Ability bias absorbed by MFRM-only estimation ($N = 8$ pilot): $\\Delta\\theta = \\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{MFRM}} - \\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{G\\text{-}MFRM}}$. The strong correlation with $\\hat{c}_n$ (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$; $N = 8$, interpret with caution) confirms that contamination inflates MFRM ability estimates for high-$c_n$ models, as predicted by the OVB analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).}\n\\label{fig:bias_absorption}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation and Ablation}\n\\label{sec:error_prop_results}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ablation_rmse}\n\\caption{Ablation on synthetic data ($N = 50$, 20 seeds). Joint estimation drives the improvement: staged estimation doubles $\\theta$-RMSE ($p < 0.001$). Continuous gating provides a moderate gain over binary ($p < 0.001$); removing judge severity $d_j$ has negligible effect on ability recovery (n.s.).}\n\\label{fig:ablation}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} validates the theoretical prediction (\\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}): below $\\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\\%; beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial. Ablation (Figure~\\ref{fig:ablation}; full results in Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation}) confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor ($2.0\\times$ $\\theta$-RMSE increase under staged estimation, $p < 0.001$) and continuous gating over binary ($c_n$-RMSE $-$40\\%, $p < 0.001$).",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "8070bfe7-c211-42ad-93c5-b0aa9261eed7",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "c3a15467-6324-438d-a707-54b9c9ab43fc",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": ", establishing Fisher information non-singularity via the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality on the $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix.",
    "newString": " and establishes Fisher information non-singularity via the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality on the $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{The G-MFRM Framework}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:confounding} established that omitting contamination from the measurement model produces directional bias in ability estimates (Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb}). We now introduce the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly estimates ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility within a single measurement framework. The key design choices, staged estimation with externally fixed exposure and a hierarchical HalfNormal prior for the contamination term, are motivated by the identifiability analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:identifiability} and the error propagation bounds in \\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}.\n\n\\subsection{Model Specification}\n\\label{sec:model_spec}\n\nG-MFRM extends the standard MFRM \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} by adding a multiplicative contamination gating term to the linear predictor (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}):\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:gmfrm}\n\\log \\frac{P(Y_{nij} \\geq k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k)} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\gamma_k + c_n \\cdot e_i,\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere all terms carry the definitions from Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}. The gating structure $c_n \\cdot e_i$ separates model-specific contamination susceptibility ($c_n \\geq 0$) from item-specific exposure ($e_i \\in [0,1]$). When $c_n = 0$ or $e_i = 0$, the gating term vanishes and Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm} reduces to the standard MFRM.\n\nWe enforce non-negativity and sparsity through a hierarchical HalfNormal prior: $\\sigma_c \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$, $c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$, $c_n = c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c$. The hierarchical structure provides adaptive shrinkage: when data support low contamination, $\\sigma_c$ contracts toward zero. The remaining priors are $\\theta_n \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $b_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $d_j \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$, and uniform on $\\gamma_k$.\n\nG-MFRM extends the Doubly-Gated IRT model of \\citet{shu2013dgirt} from binary gating to continuous exposure and multi-rater designs, and contributes the first formal identifiability conditions for gated IRT parameters (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}, Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and error propagation bounds for staged estimation (\\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}).\n\n\\subsection{Staged Estimation}\n\\label{sec:staged}\n\nJoint estimation of all parameters in Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm}, including $e_i$, would require identifying the bilinear product $c_n \\cdot e_i$ from response data alone. This creates a fundamental non-identifiability (Proposition~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}). We resolve this through a three-stage procedure that fixes exposure externally before estimating the contamination parameters.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 1: Standard MFRM.} We fit the standard MFRM (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}) via MCMC (NUTS) to obtain initial estimates $(\\hat{\\theta}, \\hat{b}, \\hat{d})$. These serve as warm-start values for the sampler in Stage~3 and improve convergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 2: Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated from external evidence independent of the response data. The primary signal is \\emph{reference model anchoring} (75\\% weight): win-rate residuals against a fixed anchor (gpt-4-0314) flag items where target models outperform difficulty-adjusted expectations. The secondary signal is \\emph{cross-model agreement} (25\\%): uniformly high scores across diverse architectures suggest exposure rather than easiness. The composite is clipped to $[0, 1]$ and fixed in Stage~3. Estimation is non-circular: $e_i$ is item-level while $c_n$ is model-level, and ablation with 100\\% anchoring yields $\\rho = 0.998$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:weight_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed, we sample $(\\theta_n, b_i, d_j, c_n)$ jointly via the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS) \\citep{betancourt2015}. The priors are as specified in \\S\\ref{sec:model_spec}. The hierarchical contamination prior decomposes as\n%\n\\begin{align}\n\\label{eq:contam_prior}\n\\sigma_c &\\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \\quad c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \\nonumber \\\\\nc_n &= c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c,\n\\end{align}\n%\nwhere $\\sigma_c$ is a shared global scale that governs the overall contamination magnitude. This non-centered parameterization improves sampler geometry by decoupling the global scale from the per-model effects.\n\nConvergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R}$ \\citep{vehtari2021rhat} and bulk effective sample size (ESS), with thresholds $\\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS$_{\\mathrm{bulk}} > 100$ following standard practice \\citep{gelman2013bda}.\\footnote{Synthetic validation with $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$ yields satisfactory convergence across all parameters. Full diagnostic tables are reported in Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}.}\n\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{forest_dj}\n\\caption{Judge severity estimates ($d_j$) with 95\\% credible intervals for the full 9-judge panel ($N = 8$ analysis). Positive values indicate stricter scoring; the severity range spans 1.34 logits. The $N = 17$ analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:setup}) uses a 6-judge subset selected for item coverage.}\n\\label{fig:judge_severity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability}\n\\label{sec:identifiability}\n\nWe establish that fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for identifying the contamination parameters. All proofs assume $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[No New Indeterminacy]\n\\label{prop:no_new}\nWhen $e_i$ is fixed externally, $c_n \\cdot e_i$ introduces no additional location or scale indeterminacy beyond the standard MFRM.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Exposure Variation Necessary]\n\\label{prop:exposure_variation}\nIf $e_i = e$ for all $i$ (constant exposure), then $c_n$ and $\\theta_n$ are not separately identifiable; hence $\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ is necessary.\n\\end{proposition}\n\nProofs are in Appendix~\\ref{app:prop_proofs}.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Sufficient Conditions for Local Identifiability]\n\\label{thm:sufficient}\nG-MFRM is locally identifiable if: (i) a connected observation design, (ii) $J \\geq 2$ judges, (iii) each model observes items with at least two distinct exposure values, and (iv) $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe proof (Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}) follows \\citet{rothenberg1971}, establishing Fisher information non-singularity via the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality on the $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix. Monte Carlo validation confirms successful $c_n$ recovery (Appendix~\\ref{app:cn_recovery}).\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation}\n\\label{sec:error_prop}\n\nTreating Stage~2 estimates $\\hat{e}_i$ as fixed creates a generated regressors problem \\citep{pagan1984}. Let $\\hat{e}_i = e_i + \\epsilon_i$ with $\\epsilon_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, \\sigma^2)$. The attenuation factor is $\\lambda(\\sigma) = \\mathrm{Var}(e_i) / [\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) + \\sigma^2]$, reaching 50\\% at $\\sigma \\approx 0.2$ in our setting ($\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) \\approx 0.04$). Empirically, $\\theta$-RMSE increases by only 5.9\\% and $c_n$-RMSE by 7.9\\% below this threshold; beyond it ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes severe (Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop}, Appendix~\\ref{app:error_prop}). We apply the Murphy--Topel correction \\citep{murphy1985} for honest credible intervals (Appendix~\\ref{app:murphy_topel}).\n\n\\subsection{Fair Scores and Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:fair_scores}\n\nThe Stage~3 posterior mean of $\\theta_n$ provides a bias-and-contamination-corrected ``fair score'' for each model. The remaining parameters serve complementary diagnostic roles: per-model $c_n$ flags candidates for training data audit, per-judge $d_j$ informs judge calibration, and per-item $e_i$ identifies items for retirement or replacement.\n",

[Director 指令] [Patch] 正文内容偏短，第 8 页右栏约一半位置就开始 References，需要扩充正文填满 8 页。

原因：Figure 5+6 合并为 figure* 后节省了约半页纵向空间。

**扩充策略**（约需增加 0.3-0.5 页内容，选择性执行直到填满）：

1. **Conclusion 扩充**：当前 Conclusion 较短。可以增加：
   - 对未来工作方向的具体讨论（如扩展到多 benchmark、处理高阶 contamination 结构、与其他 IRT 变体的整合）
   - 更详细地讨论 G-MFRM 对 LLM evaluation 实践的启示（如何改变当前 Arena 评估流程）

2. **Limitations 适度扩充**：当前 ~86 words 覆盖 3 个点，可以再加 1-2 个 limitation（如 judge 数量依赖、计算成本与扩展性），保持简洁但增加覆盖面

3. **Experiments 部分补充**：

   - 在 coverage ablation 部分增加对实际应用场景的讨论

**注意**：
- 不要水——每句话都要有实质信息
- 不要超过 8 页主文限制
- 编译确认正文恰好填满 8 页（References 从第 9 页或第 8 页底部开始）

```text
66	\paragraph{Contamination diagnostics.} G-MFRM's primary real-data contribution is interpretable per-model contamination estimation. In the $N = 8$ pilot, the contamination susceptibility profile (Figure~\ref{fig:contamination_and_bias}a) reveals a clear gradient by model age: older models show $\hat{c}_n > 4$ (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $5.60$; GPT-4-0613: $4.20$), while recent models show near-zero susceptibility (GPT-4o: $0.88$; GPT-4o-mini: $0.93$). MFRM-only estimation absorbs this contamination signal into ability estimates, inflating $\hat{\theta}$ for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (Figure~\ref{fig:contamination_and_bias}b; Spearman $\rho = 1.0$ between $\hat{c}_n$ and $\Delta\theta$, though with only $N = 8$ data points, perfect rank correlation is not statistically remarkable and should not be interpreted as a robust finding). The $N = 17$ analysis preserves the same age gradient at lower absolute scale (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $2.57$; GPT-4-0613: $3.78$), consistent with shrinkage from the larger model population. All parameters achieve $\hat{R} \leq 1.003$ with ESS $\geq 1{,}240$ in the $N = 8$ pilot (Appendices~\ref{app:item_diagnostics} and~\ref{app:ppc}).
67	
68	\paragraph{Judge severity and panel design.} Judge severity profiles vary across the panel (Figure~\ref{fig:judge_severity}), and contamination--ability separation improves with severity diversity: panels with $k \geq 4$ judges covering the severity extremes provide the most reliable diagnostic output (Appendix~\ref{app:judge_count}).
69	
70	
71	\subsection{Error Propagation and Ablation}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01T5n2xxo8izNgtRM1gYqVNT`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced G-MFRM, a Bayesian measurement model that jointly estimates model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility within a single inferential framework. The key theoretical contribution is formalizing the confounding between judge bias and benchmark contamination as omitted variable bias: omitting the contamination facet produces systematic bias in ability estimates, driven primarily by the specification error itself rather than the exposure-severity covariance (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}).
5	
6	On Arena-Hard, G-MFRM provides interpretable per-model contamination diagnostics unavailable from existing methods: older models exhibit 4--6$\times$ higher susceptibility, and MFRM-only estimation inflates ability for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits. A controlled coverage ablation ($\Delta\tau$: $-0.044 \to -0.015 \to 0.000$ as coverage increases to 100\%) demonstrates that over-correction is attributable to incomplete exposure estimation, and complete coverage eliminates the over-correction observed in partial-coverage settings. Ablation confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor: staged estimation doubles ability RMSE.
7	
8	Two practical implications emerge. First, evaluation frameworks that treat judge bias and contamination as independent incur systematic errors; future LLM evaluation should model these facets jointly. Second, the coverage--degradation finding provides an actionable quality requirement for contamination-aware evaluation. Several directions remain open: higher-rank contamination decompositions for domain-specific effects, simulation-based recalibration \citep{talts2018sbc} of $c_n$ credible intervals (71\% coverage at nominal 95\%), and scaling to larger model populations and diverse benchmarks.
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PgYyzJMcVEBTjTe3kYiN6r`</summary>

```text
1	The evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with an incomplete observation design (${\sim}$58\% fill rate); generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Posterior credible intervals for contamination susceptibility $c_n$ exhibit systematic overconfidence (71\% coverage at nominal 95\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ci_coverage}), a structural issue from the multiplicative coupling and HalfNormal boundary at $c_n = 0$ that warrants simulation-based recalibration. The rank-1 contamination structure assumes a single $c_n$ per model, whereas contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\ reasoning), motivating higher-rank decompositions in future work.
2	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig5_6_combined}\n\\caption{Contamination diagnostics ($N = 8$ pilot, 9 judges). (a)~Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ (posterior mean $\\pm$ 95\\% CI), ordered by magnitude. Older models (GPT-3.5-Turbo, GPT-4-0613) show 4--6$\\times$ higher susceptibility than recent models (GPT-4o). (b)~Ability bias absorbed by MFRM-only estimation: $\\Delta\\theta = \\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{MFRM}} - \\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{G\\text{-}MFRM}}$. The monotonic relationship (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$; $N = 8$, interpret with caution) confirms that contamination inflates MFRM ability estimates for high-$c_n$ models, as predicted by the OVB analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).}\n\\label{fig:contamination_and_bias}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nOur main experiment uses $N = 17$ target models evaluated by 6 LLM judges; a smaller $N = 8$ pilot with 9 judges validates sample-size effects. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} reports both configurations as part of the coverage analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \\citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 500 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. The evaluation uses all 500 items in a connected incomplete design (${\\sim}$58\\% fill rate of the $N \\times I \\times J$ tensor). Seventeen target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated: GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini, Qwen2.5-72B, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen3-8B, Gemma-2-9B, GPT-4-0613, GPT-3.5-Turbo, Claude-3.5-Sonnet, GPT-4-Turbo, LLaMA-3.1-70B, Gemma-2-27B, Claude-3-Opus, LLaMA-3-70B, Mixtral-8x22B, Claude-3-Haiku, and LLaMA-3-8B. Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}.\n\n\\paragraph{Judges.} Six LLM judges score responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Four provide complete item coverage; two (Gemini, Sonnet) cover ${\\sim}$63--79\\% of items; this yields an incomplete connected design. Two models serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs self-evaluation bias ($\\tau$ unchanged when excluding self-judging; Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item $e_i$ follows the two-source pipeline in \\S\\ref{sec:staged} (75\\% reference anchoring, 25\\% cross-model agreement; robust to weight configuration, $\\rho \\geq 0.998$; Appendix~\\ref{app:weight_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{MCMC.} NumPyro NUTS, 6{,}000 warmup $+$ 4{,}000 samples $\\times$ 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). Convergence: $\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$ \\citep{vehtari2021rhat, gelman2013bda}.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Item Set} & \\textbf{Items} & \\textbf{Cov.} & $\\tau_{\\mathrm{raw}}$ & $\\tau_{\\mathrm{G\\text{-}MFRM}}$ & $\\Delta\\tau$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{$N = 17$, 6 judges (main experiment)}} \\\\\nFull set & 500 & 46\\% & 0.838 & 0.794 & $-$0.044 \\\\\nFiltered & 317 & 73\\% & 0.824 & 0.809 & $-$0.015 \\\\\nExposed-only & 231 & 100\\% & 0.779 & 0.779 & \\phantom{$-$}0.000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{$N = 8$, 9 judges (pilot)}} \\\\\nFull set & 176 & -- & 0.786 & 0.857$^{\\dagger}$ & $+$0.071 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Ranking accuracy (Kendall $\\tau$ against Chatbot Arena) as a function of exposure coverage. G-MFRM over-correction decreases across all three coverage levels ($\\Delta\\tau$: $-0.044 \\to -0.015 \\to 0.000$). At full coverage, G-MFRM matches the raw average exactly; the pilot ($N = 8$) suggests positive $\\Delta\\tau$ when coverage is adequate. All seven baselines (MFRM-only, Sequential, CyclicJudge, DCR Factor, Pref.\\ Leakage) produce $\\tau$ within 0.014 of the raw average at both panel sizes. $^{\\dagger}$Bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nWe compare G-MFRM against seven baselines that address judge bias or contamination separately (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}): Raw Average, MFRM-only (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}), Sequential (MFRM then contamination adjustment), CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, CyclicJudge+discard, DCR Factor \\citep{dcr2025}, and Preference Leakage calibration \\citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\\tau$ and Spearman $\\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.\n\n\\paragraph{Synthetic validation.} On synthetic data ($N = 50$, 20 seeds), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 10^{-6}$) and improves ranking accuracy ($\\tau$: $0.922$ vs.\\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). Contamination susceptibility is recovered with Pearson $r = 0.81$.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictive fit.} In the $N = 8$ pilot, LOO cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM over MFRM-only (Expected Log Pointwise Predictive Density difference $\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, SE $= 9.4$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 and all Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:loo_comparison}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reconciling predictive fit and ranking accuracy.} The LOO improvement and the ranking degradation are not contradictory: they measure different quantities. LOO-ELPD evaluates observation-level predictive accuracy across all $(n, i, j)$ triples: it rewards the model for capturing contamination structure in individual responses. Kendall $\\tau$ evaluates aggregate system-level rankings, which are sensitive to the accuracy of per-item exposure estimates $e_i$. The separation indicates that the contamination signal is real (the model fits better), but incomplete exposure coverage causes the correction to over-correct when aggregated to rankings. The coverage ablation below confirms this interpretation.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure coverage and ranking accuracy.} G-MFRM's ranking accuracy on real data depends on exposure estimation quality. At $N = 17$, the full item set (500 items) has only 46\\% of items with non-zero exposure estimates; G-MFRM over-corrects on the remaining items, and the result is $\\Delta\\tau = -0.044$ relative to the raw average ($\\tau = 0.794$ vs.\\ $0.838$). We trace this degradation through three item subsets with increasing exposure coverage:\n%\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Full set (500 items, 46\\% $e_i > 0$): $\\Delta\\tau = -0.044$\n    \\item Filtered set (317 items, 73\\% $e_i > 0$): $\\Delta\\tau = -0.015$\n    \\item Exposed-only (231 items, 100\\% $e_i > 0$): $\\Delta\\tau = 0.000$\n\\end{itemize}\n%\nThis controlled ablation ($-0.044 \\to -0.015 \\to 0.000$) demonstrates that over-correction is attributable to incomplete exposure coverage. Two additional variants confirm the mechanism: MFRM-only on the filtered set yields $\\Delta\\tau = 0.000$, which isolates the degradation to the contamination gating term; a contamination-only variant without severity modeling yields $\\Delta\\tau = -0.118$, evidence that naive linear contamination deduction without joint calibration is catastrophically harmful.\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination diagnostics.} G-MFRM's primary real-data contribution is interpretable per-model contamination estimation. In the $N = 8$ pilot, the contamination susceptibility profile (Figure~\\ref{fig:contamination_and_bias}a) reveals a clear gradient by model age: older models show $\\hat{c}_n > 4$ (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $5.60$; GPT-4-0613: $4.20$), while recent models show near-zero susceptibility (GPT-4o: $0.88$; GPT-4o-mini: $0.93$). MFRM-only estimation absorbs this contamination signal into ability estimates, inflating $\\hat{\\theta}$ for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (Figure~\\ref{fig:contamination_and_bias}b; Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$ between $\\hat{c}_n$ and $\\Delta\\theta$, though with only $N = 8$ data points, perfect rank correlation is not statistically remarkable and should not be interpreted as a robust finding). The $N = 17$ analysis preserves the same age gradient at lower absolute scale (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $2.57$; GPT-4-0613: $3.78$), consistent with shrinkage from the larger model population. All parameters achieve $\\hat{R} \\leq 1.003$ with ESS $\\geq 1{,}240$ in the $N = 8$ pilot (Appendices~\\ref{app:item_diagnostics} and~\\ref{app:ppc}).\n\n\\paragraph{Judge severity and panel design.} Judge severity profiles vary across the panel (Figure~\\ref{fig:judge_severity}), and contamination--ability separation improves with severity diversity: panels with $k \\geq 4$ judges covering the severity extremes provide the most reliable diagnostic output (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation and Ablation}\n\\label{sec:error_prop_results}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ablation_rmse}\n\\caption{Ablation on synthetic data ($N = 50$, 20 seeds). Joint estimation drives the improvement: staged estimation doubles $\\theta$-RMSE ($p < 0.001$). Continuous gating provides a moderate gain over binary ($p < 0.001$); removing judge severity $d_j$ has negligible effect on ability recovery (n.s.).}\n\\label{fig:ablation}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} validates the theoretical prediction (\\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}): below $\\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\\%; beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial. Ablation (Figure~\\ref{fig:ablation}; full results in Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation}) confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor ($2.0\\times$ $\\theta$-RMSE increase under staged estimation, $p < 0.001$) and continuous gating over binary ($c_n$-RMSE $-$40\\%, $p < 0.001$).",

  "old_string": "Two practical implications emerge. First, evaluation frameworks that treat judge bias and contamination as independent incur systematic errors; future LLM evaluation should model these facets jointly. Second, the coverage--degradation finding provides an actionable quality requirement for contamination-aware evaluation. Several directions remain open: higher-rank contamination decompositions for domain-specific effects, simulation-based recalibration \\citep{talts2018sbc} of $c_n$ credible intervals (71\\% coverage at nominal 95\\%), and scaling to larger model populations and diverse benchmarks.",
  "new_string": "Two practical implications emerge. First, evaluation frameworks that treat judge bias and contamination as independent incur systematic errors; future LLM evaluation should model these facets jointly. Second, the coverage--degradation finding provides an actionable quality requirement for contamination-aware evaluation. For practitioners operating Arena-style evaluations, G-MFRM can serve as a post-hoc calibration layer: given pairwise judgments and exposure estimates, the model produces contamination-adjusted rankings and flags models warranting training data audits. The exposure estimation pipeline (\\S\\ref{sec:staged}) is modular, so improved contamination detectors---whether based on membership inference, output perturbation, or direct training data access---can substitute for the current reference-anchoring approach without modifying the measurement model.\n\nSeveral directions remain open: higher-rank contamination decompositions for domain-specific effects, simulation-based recalibration \\citep{talts2018sbc} of $c_n$ credible intervals (71\\% coverage at nominal 95\\%), and scaling to larger model populations and diverse benchmarks."
}

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe introduced G-MFRM, a Bayesian measurement model that jointly estimates model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility within a single inferential framework. The key theoretical contribution is formalizing the confounding between judge bias and benchmark contamination as omitted variable bias: omitting the contamination facet produces systematic bias in ability estimates, driven primarily by the specification error itself rather than the exposure-severity covariance (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\nOn Arena-Hard, G-MFRM provides interpretable per-model contamination diagnostics unavailable from existing methods: older models exhibit 4--6$\\times$ higher susceptibility, and MFRM-only estimation inflates ability for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits. A controlled coverage ablation ($\\Delta\\tau$: $-0.044 \\to -0.015 \\to 0.000$ as coverage increases to 100\\%) demonstrates that over-correction is attributable to incomplete exposure estimation, and complete coverage eliminates the over-correction observed in partial-coverage settings. Ablation confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor: staged estimation doubles ability RMSE.\n\nTwo practical implications emerge. First, evaluation frameworks that treat judge bias and contamination as independent incur systematic errors; future LLM evaluation should model these facets jointly. Second, the coverage--degradation finding provides an actionable quality requirement for contamination-aware evaluation.   "old_string": "The rank-1 contamination structure assumes a single $c_n$ per model, whereas contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\\ reasoning), motivating higher-rank decompositions in future work.",
  "new_string": "The rank-1 contamination structure assumes a single $c_n$ per model, whereas contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\\ reasoning), motivating higher-rank decompositions in future work. The judge panel comprises six models from four provider families; whether G-MFRM's severity separation generalizes to larger or more homogeneous panels (e.g., multiple versions of the same model family) is untested. Finally, exposure estimation relies on reference model anchoring with a fixed anchor (GPT-4-0314); anchor choice may introduce systematic bias in $e_i$ estimates, particularly for items where the anchor's own training data overlaps with the benchmark."
}
```

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - G-MFRM: Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model with multiplicative gating term c_n·e_i
   - OVB (Omitted Variable Bias) formalization of judge-contamination confounding
   - Three-stage estimation: Stage 1 (MFRM), Stage 2 (external exposure), Stage 3 (joint MCMC)
   - Symbol conventions: θ_n (ability), b_i (difficulty), d_j (severity), γ_k (threshold, renamed from τ_k), c_n (contamination), e_i (exposure), δ_n (OVB coefficient, renamed from γ_n)

        "content": "The evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with an incomplete observation design (${\\sim}$58\\% fill rate); generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Posterior credible intervals for contamination susceptibility $c_n$ exhibit systematic overconfidence (71\\% coverage at nominal 95\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ci_coverage}), a structural issue from the multiplicative coupling and HalfNormal boundary at $c_n = 0$ that warrants simulation-based recalibration. The rank-1 contamination structure assumes a single $c_n$ per model, whereas contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\\ reasoning), motivating higher-rank decompositions in future work. The judge panel comprises six models from four provider families; whether G-MFRM's severity separation generalizes to larger or more homogeneous panels (e.g., multiple versions of the same model family) is untested. Finally, exposure estimation relies on reference model anchoring with a fixed anchor (GPT-4-0314); anchor choice may introduce systematic bias in $e_i$ estimates, particularly for items where the anchor's own training data overlaps with the benchmark.\n",

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe introduced G-MFRM, a Bayesian measurement model that jointly estimates model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility within a single inferential framework. The key theoretical contribution is formalizing the confounding between judge bias and benchmark contamination as omitted variable bias: omitting the contamination facet produces systematic bias in ability estimates, driven primarily by the specification error itself rather than the exposure-severity covariance (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\nOn Arena-Hard, G-MFRM provides interpretable per-model contamination diagnostics unavailable from existing methods: older models exhibit 4--6$\\times$ higher susceptibility, and MFRM-only estimation inflates ability for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits. A controlled coverage ablation ($\\Delta\\tau$: $-0.044 \\to -0.015 \\to 0.000$ as coverage increases to 100\\%) demonstrates that over-correction is attributable to incomplete exposure estimation, and complete coverage eliminates the over-correction observed in partial-coverage settings. Ablation confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor: staged estimation doubles ability RMSE.\n\nTwo practical implications emerge. First, evaluation frameworks that treat judge bias and contamination as independent incur systematic errors; future LLM evaluation should model these facets jointly. Second, the coverage--degradation finding provides an actionable quality requirement for contamination-aware evaluation. For practitioners operating Arena-style evaluations, G-MFRM can serve as a post-hoc calibration layer: given pairwise judgments and exposure estimates, the model produces contamination-adjusted rankings and flags models warranting training data audits. The exposure estimation pipeline (\\S\\ref{sec:staged}) is modular, so improved contamination detectors---whether based on membership inference, output perturbation, or direct training data access---can substitute for the current reference-anchoring approach without modifying the measurement model.\n\nSeveral directions remain open: higher-rank contamination decompositions for domain-specific effects, simulation-based recalibration \\citep{talts2018sbc} of $c_n$ credible intervals (71\\% coverage at nominal 95\\%), and scaling to larger model populations and diverse benchmarks.",
        "numLines": 10,
        "startLine": 1,

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig5_6_combined}\n\\caption{Contamination diagnostics ($N = 8$ pilot, 9 judges). (a)~Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ (posterior mean $\\pm$ 95\\% CI), ordered by magnitude. Older models (GPT-3.5-Turbo, GPT-4-0613) show 4--6$\\times$ higher susceptibility than recent models (GPT-4o). (b)~Ability bias absorbed by MFRM-only estimation: $\\Delta\\theta = \\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{MFRM}} - \\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{G\\text{-}MFRM}}$. The monotonic relationship (Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$; $N = 8$, interpret with caution) confirms that contamination inflates MFRM ability estimates for high-$c_n$ models, as predicted by the OVB analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).}\n\\label{fig:contamination_and_bias}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\nOur main experiment uses $N = 17$ target models evaluated by 6 LLM judges; a smaller $N = 8$ pilot with 9 judges validates sample-size effects. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} reports both configurations as part of the coverage analysis.\n\n\\paragraph{Data and models.} We evaluate on Arena-Hard \\citep{li2024arenahard}, a benchmark of 500 multi-turn conversational items selected for high separability among frontier models. The evaluation uses all 500 items in a connected incomplete design (${\\sim}$58\\% fill rate of the $N \\times I \\times J$ tensor). Seventeen target models spanning proprietary and open-source families are evaluated: GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini, Qwen2.5-72B, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen3-8B, Gemma-2-9B, GPT-4-0613, GPT-3.5-Turbo, Claude-3.5-Sonnet, GPT-4-Turbo, LLaMA-3.1-70B, Gemma-2-27B, Claude-3-Opus, LLaMA-3-70B, Mixtral-8x22B, Claude-3-Haiku, and LLaMA-3-8B. Ground-truth rankings are derived from Chatbot Arena human preference data \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}.\n\n\\paragraph{Judges.} Six LLM judges score responses in position-swapped pairwise mode: Claude Sonnet 4.6, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Gemini-2.5-Pro, LLaMA-3.3-70B, Qwen2.5-72B, and Qwen3-235B. Four provide complete item coverage; two (Gemini, Sonnet) cover ${\\sim}$63--79\\% of items; this yields an incomplete connected design. Two models serve as both judge and target; $d_j$ absorbs self-evaluation bias ($\\tau$ unchanged when excluding self-judging; Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}).\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure estimation.} Per-item $e_i$ follows the two-source pipeline in \\S\\ref{sec:staged} (75\\% reference anchoring, 25\\% cross-model agreement; robust to weight configuration, $\\rho \\geq 0.998$; Appendix~\\ref{app:weight_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{MCMC.} NumPyro NUTS, 6{,}000 warmup $+$ 4{,}000 samples $\\times$ 4 chains (target acceptance 0.95). Convergence: $\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$ \\citep{vehtari2021rhat, gelman2013bda}.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main_results}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Item Set} & \\textbf{Items} & \\textbf{Cov.} & $\\tau_{\\mathrm{raw}}$ & $\\tau_{\\mathrm{G\\text{-}MFRM}}$ & $\\Delta\\tau$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{$N = 17$, 6 judges (main experiment)}} \\\\\nFull set & 500 & 46\\% & 0.838 & 0.794 & $-$0.044 \\\\\nFiltered & 317 & 73\\% & 0.824 & 0.809 & $-$0.015 \\\\\nExposed-only & 231 & 100\\% & 0.779 & 0.779 & \\phantom{$-$}0.000 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{$N = 8$, 9 judges (pilot)}} \\\\\nFull set & 176 & -- & 0.786 & 0.857$^{\\dagger}$ & $+$0.071 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Ranking accuracy (Kendall $\\tau$ against Chatbot Arena) as a function of exposure coverage. G-MFRM over-correction decreases across all three coverage levels ($\\Delta\\tau$: $-0.044 \\to -0.015 \\to 0.000$). At full coverage, G-MFRM matches the raw average exactly; the pilot ($N = 8$) suggests positive $\\Delta\\tau$ when coverage is adequate. All seven baselines (MFRM-only, Sequential, CyclicJudge, DCR Factor, Pref.\\ Leakage) produce $\\tau$ within 0.014 of the raw average at both panel sizes. $^{\\dagger}$Bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\end{table}\n\nWe compare G-MFRM against seven baselines that address judge bias or contamination separately (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}): Raw Average, MFRM-only (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}), Sequential (MFRM then contamination adjustment), CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, CyclicJudge+discard, DCR Factor \\citep{dcr2025}, and Preference Leakage calibration \\citep{preference_leakage2026}. All methods are evaluated by Kendall $\\tau$ and Spearman $\\rho$ against Chatbot Arena human rankings.\n\n\\paragraph{Synthetic validation.} On synthetic data ($N = 50$, 20 seeds), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 10^{-6}$) and improves ranking accuracy ($\\tau$: $0.922$ vs.\\ $0.839$, Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). Contamination susceptibility is recovered with Pearson $r = 0.81$.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictive fit.} In the $N = 8$ pilot, LOO cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM over MFRM-only (Expected Log Pointwise Predictive Density difference $\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, SE $= 9.4$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 and all Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:loo_comparison}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reconciling predictive fit and ranking accuracy.} The LOO improvement and the ranking degradation are not contradictory: they measure different quantities. LOO-ELPD evaluates observation-level predictive accuracy across all $(n, i, j)$ triples: it rewards the model for capturing contamination structure in individual responses. Kendall $\\tau$ evaluates aggregate system-level rankings, which are sensitive to the accuracy of per-item exposure estimates $e_i$. The separation indicates that the contamination signal is real (the model fits better), but incomplete exposure coverage causes the correction to over-correct when aggregated to rankings. The coverage ablation below confirms this interpretation.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure coverage and ranking accuracy.} G-MFRM's ranking accuracy on real data depends on exposure estimation quality. At $N = 17$, the full item set (500 items) has only 46\\% of items with non-zero exposure estimates; G-MFRM over-corrects on the remaining items, and the result is $\\Delta\\tau = -0.044$ relative to the raw average ($\\tau = 0.794$ vs.\\ $0.838$). We trace this degradation through three item subsets with increasing exposure coverage:\n%\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item Full set (500 items, 46\\% $e_i > 0$): $\\Delta\\tau = -0.044$\n    \\item Filtered set (317 items, 73\\% $e_i > 0$): $\\Delta\\tau = -0.015$\n    \\item Exposed-only (231 items, 100\\% $e_i > 0$): $\\Delta\\tau = 0.000$\n\\end{itemize}\n%\nThis controlled ablation ($-0.044 \\to -0.015 \\to 0.000$) demonstrates that over-correction is attributable to incomplete exposure coverage. Two additional variants confirm the mechanism: MFRM-only on the filtered set yields $\\Delta\\tau = 0.000$, which isolates the degradation to the contamination gating term; a contamination-only variant without severity modeling yields $\\Delta\\tau = -0.118$, evidence that naive linear contamination deduction without joint calibration is catastrophically harmful.\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination diagnostics.} G-MFRM's primary real-data contribution is interpretable per-model contamination estimation. In the $N = 8$ pilot, the contamination susceptibility profile (Figure~\\ref{fig:contamination_and_bias}a) reveals a clear gradient by model age: older models show $\\hat{c}_n > 4$ (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $5.60$; GPT-4-0613: $4.20$), while recent models show near-zero susceptibility (GPT-4o: $0.88$; GPT-4o-mini: $0.93$). MFRM-only estimation absorbs this contamination signal into ability estimates, inflating $\\hat{\\theta}$ for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (Figure~\\ref{fig:contamination_and_bias}b; Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$ between $\\hat{c}_n$ and $\\Delta\\theta$, though with only $N = 8$ data points, perfect rank correlation is not statistically remarkable and should not be interpreted as a robust finding). The $N = 17$ analysis preserves the same age gradient at lower absolute scale (GPT-3.5-Turbo: $2.57$; GPT-4-0613: $3.78$), consistent with shrinkage from the larger model population. All parameters achieve $\\hat{R} \\leq 1.003$ with ESS $\\geq 1{,}240$ in the $N = 8$ pilot (Appendices~\\ref{app:item_diagnostics} and~\\ref{app:ppc}).\n\n\\paragraph{Judge severity and panel design.} Judge severity profiles vary across the panel (Figure~\\ref{fig:judge_severity}), and contamination--ability separation improves with severity diversity: panels with $k \\geq 4$ judges covering the severity extremes provide the most reliable diagnostic output (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}). Leave-one-judge-out analysis identifies three individually necessary judges---those at the strict and lenient extremes of the $d_j$ distribution---whose removal reduces G-MFRM $\\tau$ to the baseline. This finding suggests that judge panels should prioritize scoring tendency diversity over judge count alone.\n\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation and Ablation}\n\\label{sec:error_prop_results}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ablation_rmse}\n\\caption{Ablation on synthetic data ($N = 50$, 20 seeds). Joint estimation drives the improvement: staged estimation doubles $\\theta$-RMSE ($p < 0.001$). Continuous gating provides a moderate gain over binary ($p < 0.001$); removing judge severity $d_j$ has negligible effect on ability recovery (n.s.).}\n\\label{fig:ablation}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:error_prop} validates the theoretical prediction (\\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}): below $\\sigma = 0.2$, ability RMSE increases by 5.9\\% and contamination RMSE by 7.9\\%; beyond the threshold ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes substantial. Ablation (Figure~\\ref{fig:ablation}; full results in Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation}) confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor ($2.0\\times$ $\\theta$-RMSE increase under staged estimation, $p < 0.001$) and continuous gating over binary ($c_n$-RMSE $-$40\\%, $p < 0.001$).",
        "numLines": 81,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 81
      }
    },

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "90c2c57f-653a-4250-845a-37925b5aa6c7",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{The G-MFRM Framework}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:confounding} established that omitting contamination from the measurement model produces directional bias in ability estimates (Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb}). We now introduce the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly estimates ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility within a single measurement framework. The key design choices, staged estimation with externally fixed exposure and a hierarchical HalfNormal prior for the contamination term, are motivated by the identifiability analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:identifiability} and the error propagation bounds in \\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}.\n\n\\subsection{Model Specification}\n\\label{sec:model_spec}\n\nG-MFRM extends the standard MFRM \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} by adding a multiplicative contamination gating term to the linear predictor (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}):\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:gmfrm}\n\\log \\frac{P(Y_{nij} \\geq k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k)} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\gamma_k + c_n \\cdot e_i,\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere all terms carry the definitions from Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}. The gating structure $c_n \\cdot e_i$ separates model-specific contamination susceptibility ($c_n \\geq 0$) from item-specific exposure ($e_i \\in [0,1]$). When $c_n = 0$ or $e_i = 0$, the gating term vanishes and Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm} reduces to the standard MFRM.\n\nWe enforce non-negativity and sparsity through a hierarchical HalfNormal prior: $\\sigma_c \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$, $c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$, $c_n = c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c$. The hierarchical structure provides adaptive shrinkage: when data support low contamination, $\\sigma_c$ contracts toward zero. The remaining priors are $\\theta_n \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $b_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $d_j \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$, and uniform on $\\gamma_k$.\n\nG-MFRM extends the Doubly-Gated IRT model of \\citet{shu2013dgirt} from binary gating to continuous exposure and multi-rater designs, and contributes the first formal identifiability conditions for gated IRT parameters (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}, Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and error propagation bounds for staged estimation (\\S\\ref{sec:error_prop}).\n\n\\subsection{Staged Estimation}\n\\label{sec:staged}\n\nJoint estimation of all parameters in Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm}, including $e_i$, would require identifying the bilinear product $c_n \\cdot e_i$ from response data alone. This creates a fundamental non-identifiability (Proposition~\\ref{prop:exposure_variation}). We resolve this through a three-stage procedure that fixes exposure externally before estimating the contamination parameters.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 1: Standard MFRM.} We fit the standard MFRM (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}) via MCMC (NUTS) to obtain initial estimates $(\\hat{\\theta}, \\hat{b}, \\hat{d})$. These serve as warm-start values for the sampler in Stage~3 and improve convergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 2: Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated from external evidence independent of the response data. The primary signal is \\emph{reference model anchoring} (75\\% weight): win-rate residuals against a fixed anchor (gpt-4-0314) flag items where target models outperform difficulty-adjusted expectations. The secondary signal is \\emph{cross-model agreement} (25\\%): uniformly high scores across diverse architectures suggest exposure rather than easiness. The composite is clipped to $[0, 1]$ and fixed in Stage~3. Estimation is non-circular: $e_i$ is item-level while $c_n$ is model-level, and ablation with 100\\% anchoring yields $\\rho = 0.998$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:weight_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed, we sample $(\\theta_n, b_i, d_j, c_n)$ jointly via the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS) \\citep{betancourt2015}. The priors are as specified in \\S\\ref{sec:model_spec}. The hierarchical contamination prior decomposes as\n%\n\\begin{align}\n\\label{eq:contam_prior}\n\\sigma_c &\\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \\quad c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\sim \\text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \\nonumber \\\\\nc_n &= c^{\\mathrm{raw}}_n \\cdot \\sigma_c,\n\\end{align}\n%\nwhere $\\sigma_c$ is a shared global scale that governs the overall contamination magnitude. This non-centered parameterization improves sampler geometry by decoupling the global scale from the per-model effects.\n\nConvergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R}$ \\citep{vehtari2021rhat} and bulk effective sample size (ESS), with thresholds $\\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS$_{\\mathrm{bulk}} > 100$ following standard practice \\citep{gelman2013bda}.\\footnote{Synthetic validation with $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$ yields satisfactory convergence across all parameters. Full diagnostic tables are reported in Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}.}\n\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{forest_dj}\n\\caption{Judge severity estimates ($d_j$) with 95\\% credible intervals for the full 9-judge panel ($N = 8$ analysis). Positive values indicate stricter scoring; the severity range spans 1.34 logits. The $N = 17$ analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:setup}) uses a 6-judge subset selected for item coverage.}\n\\label{fig:judge_severity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability}\n\\label{sec:identifiability}\n\nWe establish that fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for identifying the contamination parameters. All proofs assume $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$.\n\n\\begin{proposition}[No New Indeterminacy]\n\\label{prop:no_new}\nWhen $e_i$ is fixed externally, $c_n \\cdot e_i$ introduces no additional location or scale indeterminacy beyond the standard MFRM.\n\\end{proposition}\n\n\\begin{proposition}[Exposure Variation Necessary]\n\\label{prop:exposure_variation}\nIf $e_i = e$ for all $i$ (constant exposure), then $c_n$ and $\\theta_n$ are not separately identifiable; hence $\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ is necessary.\n\\end{proposition}\n\nProofs are in Appendix~\\ref{app:prop_proofs}.\n\n\\begin{theorem}[Sufficient Conditions for Local Identifiability]\n\\label{thm:sufficient}\nG-MFRM is locally identifiable if: (i) a connected observation design, (ii) $J \\geq 2$ judges, (iii) each model observes items with at least two distinct exposure values, and (iv) $\\sum_n \\theta_n = 0$.\n\\end{theorem}\n\nThe proof (Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}) follows \\citet{rothenberg1971} and establishes Fisher information non-singularity via the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality on the $(\\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix. Monte Carlo validation confirms successful $c_n$ recovery (Appendix~\\ref{app:cn_recovery}).\n\n\\subsection{Error Propagation}\n\\label{sec:error_prop}\n\nTreating Stage~2 estimates $\\hat{e}_i$ as fixed creates a generated regressors problem \\citep{pagan1984}. Let $\\hat{e}_i = e_i + \\epsilon_i$ with $\\epsilon_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0, \\sigma^2)$. The attenuation factor is $\\lambda(\\sigma) = \\mathrm{Var}(e_i) / [\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) + \\sigma^2]$, reaching 50\\% at $\\sigma \\approx 0.2$ in our setting ($\\mathrm{Var}(e_i) \\approx 0.04$). Empirically, $\\theta$-RMSE increases by only 5.9\\% and $c_n$-RMSE by 7.9\\% below this threshold; beyond it ($\\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes severe (Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop}, Appendix~\\ref{app:error_prop}). We apply the Murphy--Topel correction \\citep{murphy1985} for honest credible intervals (Appendix~\\ref{app:murphy_topel}).\n\n\\subsection{Fair Scores and Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:fair_scores}\n\nThe Stage~3 posterior mean of $\\theta_n$ provides a bias-and-contamination-corrected ``fair score'' for each model. The remaining parameters serve complementary diagnostic roles: per-model $c_n$ flags candidates for training data audit, per-judge $d_j$ informs judge calibration, and per-item $e_i$ identifies items for retirement or replacement.\n",
        "numLines": 84,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 84
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/method.tex"
  },

The evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with an incomplete observation design (${\sim}$58\% fill rate); generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Posterior credible intervals for contamination susceptibility $c_n$ exhibit systematic overconfidence (71\% coverage at nominal 95\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ci_coverage}), a structural issue from the multiplicative coupling and HalfNormal boundary at $c_n = 0$ that warrants simulation-based recalibration. 

Here is the next thinking you need to rewrite:
The rank-1 contamination structure assumes a single $c_n$ per model, whereas contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\ reasoning), motivating higher-rank decompositions in future work. The judge panel comprises six models from four provider families; whether G-MFRM's severity separation generalizes to larger or more homogeneous panels (e.g., multiple versions of the same model family) is untested. Finally, exposure estimation relies on reference model anchoring with a fixed anchor (GPT-4-0314); anchor choice may introduce systematic bias in $e_i$ estimates, particularly for items where the anchor's own training data overlaps with the benchmark.
```

````text
The conclusion content is:

```latex
\section{Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}

We introduced G-MFRM, a Bayesian measurement model that jointly estimates model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility within a single inferential framework. The key theoretical contribution is formalizing the confounding between judge bias and benchmark contamination as omitted variable bias: omitting the contamination facet produces systematic bias in ability estimates, driven primarily by the specification error itself rather than the exposure-severity covariance (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}).

On Arena-Hard, G-MFRM provides interpretable per-model contamination diagnostics unavailable from existing methods: older models exhibit 4--6$\times$ higher susceptibility, and MFRM-only estimation inflates ability for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits. 

Here is the next thinking you need to rewrite:
A controlled coverage ablation ($\Delta\tau$: $-0.044 \to -0.015 \to 0.000$ as coverage increases to 100\%) demonstrates that over-correction is attributable to incomplete exposure estimation, and complete coverage eliminates the over-correction observed in partial-coverage settings. Ablation confirms joint estimation as the dominant contributor: staged estimation doubles ability RMSE.

Two practical implications emerge. First, evaluation frameworks that treat judge bias and contamination as independent incur systematic errors; future LLM evaluation should model these facets jointly. Second, the coverage--degradation finding provides an actionable quality requirement for contamination-aware evaluation. For practitioners operating Arena-style evaluations, G-MFRM can serve as a post-hoc calibration
```

Compress to 1-3 sentences of plain prose. If the thinking contains code, describe what it does rather than what is written -- e.g. "I'm building the layout with a gradient background and animated elements" instead of reproducing the code verbatim. Do not repeat information already in your current rewritten thinking. If your output so far ends with code, do not continue that pattern -- write only in prose from this point forward.

If your current rewritten thinking already describes code being written and this next chunk is a continuation of that same code, output ONLY a short phrase (2-5 words) ending in "..." that describes what the code is producing (e.g. "Writing SVG code...", "Generating markdown table...", "Still writing styles..."). Do not write a full sentence or re-describe the code.

Your output is shown directly to the user as Claude's live thinking. Always write as natural inner monologue. Never mention that you are summarizing, rewriting, or that the thinking is incomplete or cut off. If the content seems fragmented or is raw data, just describe what the content is about naturally (e.g. "Now I'm examining the error rates across these tools."). Never say "I cannot complete this thought" or "this doesn't form a coherent thought".

---

The ablation study shows that over-correction stems from incomplete exposure estimation, and reaching full coverage eliminates this bias—joint estimation proves far more effective than staged approaches, cutting ability RMSE in half. This points to two key takeaways: evaluation systems must model judge bias and contamination jointly rather than independently, and maintaining complete coverage becomes a critical quality standard for contamination-aware benchmarking. For practitioners running Arena-style evaluations, G-MFRM offers a practical post-hoc calibration method.

The exposure estimation pipeline is modular, so better contamination detection approaches—whether through membership inference, output perturbation, or direct data access—can be swapped in without changing the core measurement framework. Several open questions remain around higher-order contamination decompositions, improving the credible interval coverage through simulation-based recalibration, and extending the approach to larger model populations and varied benchmarks. I'm working through the space constraints in this section. Removing the modular pipeline sentence entirely seems like the right call since the core ideas about practical implications and post-hoc calibration are already covered elsewhere. This should free up enough room without losing the essential content the Director wanted expanded.

_source line 955 · 2026-05-25T19:01:29.700Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01VANdnoWmkWkUMURbS8nx2k`</summary>

```text
1	The evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with an incomplete observation design (${\sim}$58\% fill rate); generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Posterior credible intervals for contamination susceptibility $c_n$ exhibit systematic overconfidence (71\% coverage at nominal 95\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ci_coverage}), a structural issue from the multiplicative coupling and HalfNormal boundary at $c_n = 0$ that warrants simulation-based recalibration. The rank-1 contamination structure assumes a single $c_n$ per model, whereas contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\ reasoning), motivating higher-rank decompositions in future work. The judge panel comprises six models from four provider families; generalization to more homogeneous panels is untested. Finally, the fixed anchor (GPT-4-0314) in exposure estimation may bias $e_i$ for items overlapping with the anchor's training data.
2	
```

_source line 973 · 2026-05-25T19:01:59.231Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018qrraMM2YJZBFFthgjgDHb`</summary>

```text
1	The evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with an incomplete observation design (${\sim}$58\% fill rate); generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Posterior credible intervals for contamination susceptibility $c_n$ exhibit systematic overconfidence (71\% coverage at nominal 95\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ci_coverage}), a structural issue from the multiplicative coupling and HalfNormal boundary at $c_n = 0$ that warrants simulation-based recalibration. The rank-1 contamination structure assumes a single $c_n$ per model, whereas contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\ reasoning), motivating higher-rank decompositions in future work. The judge panel comprises six models from four provider families; generalization to more homogeneous panels is untested. Finally, exposure estimation relies on a fixed anchor (GPT-4-0314), whose own training data overlap may bias $e_i$.
2	
```
